Main article: [Reaganomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reaganomics)

Reagan advocated a *[laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire)* philosophy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaraagac2002113-196] and promoted a set of [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) reforms dubbed "Reaganomics", which included [monetarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monetarism) and [supply-side economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply-side_economics).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2013221Gerstle2022150Roy2012155-197]

#### Taxation

| icon | This section **is missing information** about analysis. Please expand the section to include this informationto include this information or [by making an edit request](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Talk%3ARonald_Reagan\&preload=Template%3ASubmit+an+edit+request%2Fpreload\&action=edit\&section=new\&editintro=Template%3AEdit+extended-protected%2Feditintro\&preloadtitle=Extended-confirmed-protected+edit+request+on+3+October+2026\&preloadparams%5B%5D=edit+extended-protected\&preloadparams%5B%5D=Ronald+Reagan). Further details may exist on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Ronald_Reagan#). *(November 2023)* |
| --- | --- |

Reagan addressing the nation from the Oval Office on tax reduction legislation, 1981

Reagan outlining his plan for tax cuts, 1981

Reagan worked with the [boll weevil Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boll_weevil_Democrats) to pass tax and budget legislation in a Congress led by [Tip O'Neill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tip_O'Neill), a liberal who strongly criticized Reaganomics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2001100Pemberton199899–102-198][^cite_note-200] He lifted federal oil and gasoline price controls on January 28, 1981,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraetz201234-201] and in August, he signed the [Economic Recovery Tax Act of 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Recovery_Tax_Act_of_1981)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998103-202] to dramatically lower federal [income tax rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States) and require exemptions and brackets to be indexed for inflation starting in 1985.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESteuerle199242-203] Amid growing concerns about [the mounting federal debt](322221.md), Reagan signed the [Tax Equity and Fiscal Responsibility Act of 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Equity_and_Fiscal_Responsibility_Act_of_1982),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998127–128-204] one of the eleven times Reagan raised taxes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartlett201244-205] The bill doubled [the federal cigarette tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cigarette_taxes_in_the_United_States), rescinded a portion of the corporate tax cuts from the 1981 tax bill,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201562-206] and according to [Paul Krugman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Krugman), "a third of the 1981 cut" overall.[^cite_note-207] Many of his supporters condemned the bill, but Reagan defended his preservation of cuts on individual income tax rates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998128-208] By 1983, the amount of federal tax had fallen for all or most taxpayers, with taxes for higher-income people decreasing the most.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201563-209]

The [Tax Reform Act of 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Reform_Act_of_1986) reduced the number of tax brackets and top tax rate, and almost doubled [personal exemptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_exemption).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998145-210]

Reagan believed [the tax cuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_tax_cuts) would not have increased the deficit as long as there was enough economic growth and spending cuts. His policies proposed that economic growth would occur when the tax cuts spurred investments. This theoretical relationship has been illustrated by some with the controversial [Laffer curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laffer_curve).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton199896Woodard2012119-211] Critics labeled this "[trickle-down economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trickle-down_economics)", the belief that tax policies that benefit the wealthy will spread to the poor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000222-212] [Milton Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_Friedman) and [Robert Mundell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mundell) argued that these policies invigorated America's economy and contributed to the [economic boom of the 1990s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990s_United_States_boom).[^cite_note-213]

#### Inflation and unemployment

Line charts showing Bureau of Labor Statistics and Federal Reserve Economic Data information on the monthly unemployment, inflation, and interest rates from January 1981 to January 1989

Monthly unemployment, inflation, and interest rates from January 1981 to January 1989 according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics and [Federal Reserve Economic Data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Economic_Data)

Reagan took office in the midst of [stagflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagflation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2013221-214] The economy briefly experienced growth before plunging into a recession in July 1981.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201590-215] As Federal Reserve chairman, [Paul Volcker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Volcker) fought inflation by pursuing [a tight money policy of high interest rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015318-216] which restricted lending and investment, raised unemployment, and temporarily reduced economic growth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201589–90-217] In December 1982, the [Bureau of Labor Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics) (BLS) measured the unemployment rate at 10.8 percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeGrasse198314-218] Around the same time, economic activity [began to rise until its end in 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_economic_expansions_in_the_United_States), setting the record for the (then) longest peacetime expansion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESinai19921-219][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000235-220][^cite_note-221] In 1983, the recession ended[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015452-222] and Reagan nominated Volcker to a second term in fear of damaging confidence in the economic recovery.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015668-223]

Reagan appointed [Alan Greenspan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Greenspan) to succeed Volcker in 1987. Greenspan raised interest rates in another attempt to curb inflation, setting off the [Black Monday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Monday_\(1987\)) stock market crash, although the markets eventually recovered.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015669–671-224] By 1989, the BLS measured unemployment at 5.3 percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELi2013219-225] The inflation rate dropped from 12 percent during the 1980 election to under 5 percent in 1989. Likewise, the interest rate dropped from 15 percent to under 10 percent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998206-226] Yet, not all shared equally in the economic recovery, and both [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_the_United_States)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005166–167-227] and the number of [homeless individuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_in_the_United_States) increased during the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow2015144–145-228] Critics have contended that a majority of the jobs created during this decade paid the minimum wage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998207-229]

#### Government spending

In 1981, in an effort to keep it solvent, Reagan approved a plan for cuts to Social Security. He later backed off due to public backlash.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015300–303-230] He then created the [Greenspan Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenspan_Commission) to keep Social Security financially secure, and in 1983 he signed amendments to raise both the program's payroll taxes and retirement age for benefits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005163–164-231] He had signed the [Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnibus_Budget_Reconciliation_Act_of_1981) to cut funding for [federal assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_assistance) such as food stamps, [unemployment benefits](378430.md), [subsidized housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidized_housing) and the [Aid to Families with Dependent Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aid_to_Families_with_Dependent_Children),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998141-232] and would discontinue the [Comprehensive Employment and Training Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Employment_and_Training_Act).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005157-233] On the other side, defense spending doubled between 1981 and 1985.[^cite_note-Bowman_2004-161] During Reagan's presidency, [Project Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Socrates) operated within the [Defense Intelligence Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_Intelligence_Agency) to discover why the United States was unable to maintain its economic competitiveness. According to program director Michael Sekora, their findings helped the country surpass the Soviets in terms of missile defense technology.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFialka19998-235]

#### Deregulation

Reagan sought to loosen federal regulation of economic activities, and he appointed key officials who shared this agenda. [William Leuchtenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Leuchtenburg) writes that by 1986, the Reagan administration eliminated almost half of the federal regulations that had existed in 1981.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeuchtenburg2015602–604-236] The 1982 [Garn–St. Germain Depository Institutions Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garn%E2%80%93St._Germain_Depository_Institutions_Act) deregulated [savings and loan associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savings_and_loan_association) by letting them make a variety of loans and investments outside of real estate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998130-237] After the bill's passage, savings and loans associations engaged in riskier activities, and the leaders of some institutions embezzled funds. The administration's inattentiveness toward the industry contributed to the [savings and loan crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savings_and_loan_crisis) and costly bailouts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005175-238]

#### Deficits

The deficits were exacerbated by the early 1980s recession, which cut into federal revenue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeuchtenburg2015605–606-239] The national debt tripled between the fiscal years of 1980 and 1989, and the national debt as a percentage of the gross domestic product rose from 33 percent in 1981 to 53 percent by 1989. During his time in office, Reagan never fulfilled his 1980 campaign promise of submitting a [balanced budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget). The United States borrowed heavily to cover newly spawned federal budget deficits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005158–159Woodard2012132-240] Reagan described the tripled debt the "greatest disappointment of his presidency".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2001128-241] [Jeffrey Frankel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Frankel) opined that the deficits were a major reason why Reagan's successor, Bush, reneged on [his campaign promise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Read_my_lips:_no_new_taxes) by raising taxes through the [Budget Enforcement Act of 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_Enforcement_Act_of_1990).[^cite_note-242]

### Assassination attempt

Main article: [Attempted assassination of Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Ronald_Reagan)

Ronald Reagan waves his hand as he walks out of the Washington Hilton. Surrounding him are secret service agents, policemen, press secretary James Brady, and aide Michael Deaver.

Reagan (center) waves just before he is shot on March 30, 1981.

On March 30, 1981, Reagan was shot by [John Hinckley Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hinckley_Jr.) outside the [Washington Hilton](11732002.md). Although "right on the margin of death" upon arrival at [George Washington University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_University_Hospital), Reagan underwent surgery and recovered quickly from a broken rib, punctured lung, and internal bleeding. Later, Reagan came to believe that God had spared his life "for a chosen mission".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKengor2004210-243]

### Supreme Court appointments

Main article: [Ronald Reagan Supreme Court candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Supreme_Court_candidates)

Reagan appointed three Associate Justices to the [Supreme Court of the United States](31737.md): [Sandra Day O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_Day_O'Connor) in 1981, which fulfilled a campaign promise to name the first female justice to the Court, [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia) in 1986, and [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy) in 1988. He also elevated [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist) from Associate Justice to Chief Justice in 1986.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998147–148-244] The direction of the Supreme Court's reshaping has been described as conservative.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShull199344-245][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998148-246]

### Public sector labor union fights

Ronald Reagan speaks to the press in the Rose Garden about the Professional Air Traffic Controllers Organization strike.

Reagan making a statement to the press regarding the air traffic controllers strike, 1981

Early in August 1981, the [Professional Air Traffic Controllers Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_Air_Traffic_Controllers_Organization_\(1968\)) (PATCO) [went on strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Professional_Air_Traffic_Controllers_Organization_strike), violating a federal law prohibiting government unions from striking.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201585–86-247] On August 3, Reagan said that he would fire air traffic controllers if they did not return to work within 48 hours; according to him, 38 percent did not return. On August 13, Reagan fired roughly 12,000 striking air traffic controllers who ignored his order.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998107-248] He used military controllers[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005158-249] and supervisors to handle the nation's commercial air traffic until new controllers could be hired and trained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201586-250] The breaking of the PATCO strike demoralized organized labor, and the number of strikes fell greatly in the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005158-249] With the assent of Reagan's sympathetic [National Labor Relations Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Labor_Relations_Board) appointees, many companies also won wage and benefit cutbacks from unions, especially in the manufacturing sector.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow201588-251] During Reagan's presidency, the share of employees who were part of a labor union dropped from approximately one-fourth of the total workforce to approximately one-sixth of the total workforce.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005170-252]

### Civil rights

Ronald Reagan at the signing ceremony for Martin Luther King Jr. Day legislation in the Rose Garden. Coretta Scott King, George H. W. Bush, Howard Baker, Bob Dole, Jack Kemp, Samuel Pierce, and Katie Hall looking on.

Reagan signing the Passage of Martin Luther King Jr. Day, 1983

Despite Reagan having opposed the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000458-33] which he long-deemed "humiliating to the South",[^cite_note-253] the bill was extended for 25 years in 1982.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyssar2009213-254] He initially opposed the establishment of Martin Luther King Jr. Day, believing that the momentum for establishing the holiday was "based on an image, not reality," but signed [a bill to create the holiday in 1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passage_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr._Day) after it passed both houses of Congress with veto-proof margins.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000461-256] In 1984, he signed legislation intended to impose fines for [fair housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_housing) discrimination offenses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShull199356–57-257] In March 1988, Reagan vetoed the [Civil Rights Restoration Act of 1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Restoration_Act_of_1987), but Congress overrode his veto. He had argued that the bill unreasonably increased the federal government's power and undermined the rights of churches and business owners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000462–463-258] In doing so, Reagan was the first US president to veto civil rights legislation since [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson) vetoed the [Civil Rights Act of 1866](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1866), which was also overridden by Congress.[^cite_note-259] Later in September, legislation was passed to correct loopholes in the [Fair Housing Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Housing_Act_of_1968).[^cite_note-Thomas-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShull199314-261]

Early in his presidency, Reagan appointed [Clarence M. Pendleton Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_M._Pendleton_Jr.), known for his opposition to affirmative action and equal pay for men and women, as chair of the [United States Commission on Civil Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Commission_on_Civil_Rights). Pendleton and Reagan's subsequent appointees greatly eroded the enforcement of civil rights law, arousing the ire of civil rights advocates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShull1993114–116-262] In 1987, Reagan unsuccessfully [nominated Robert Bork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork_Supreme_Court_nomination) to the Supreme Court as a way to achieve his civil rights policy that could not be fulfilled during his presidency; his administration had opposed [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action), particularly in education, federal assistance programs, housing and employment,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmaker1988157–159-263] but Reagan reluctantly continued these policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005171-264] In housing, Reagan's administration saw considerably fewer fair housing cases filed than the three previous administrations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmaker198892–95-265]

### War on drugs

Main article: [War on drugs](1181646.md)

Ronald Reagan with Nancy Reagan, Paula Hawkins, Charles Rangel and Benjamin Gilman for the signing ceremony for the Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1986 in the East Room, 1986

Reagan signing the Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1986

In response to concerns about the increasing [crack epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crack_epidemic), Reagan intensified the war on drugs in 1982.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander20105-266] While the American public did not see drugs as an important issue then, the FBI, [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md) and the [United States Department of Defense](7279897.md) all increased their [anti-drug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-drug) funding immensely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander201049-267] Reagan's administration publicized the campaign to gain support after crack became widespread in 1985.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander201052-268] Reagan signed the [Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Drug_Abuse_Act_of_1986) and [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Drug_Abuse_Act_of_1988) to specify penalties for drug offenses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlexander201053-269] Both bills have been criticized in the years since for promoting [racial disparities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_the_war_on_drugs).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESirin201191–96-270] Nancy Reagan founded the "[Just Say No](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just_Say_No)" campaign to discourage others from engaging in [recreational drug use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recreational_drug_use) and raise awareness about the dangers of drugs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012163–164-271] A 1988 study showed 39 percent of high school seniors using illegal drugs compared to 53 percent in 1980,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon200010-272] but [Scott Lilienfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Lilienfeld) and Hal Arkowitz say that the success of these types of campaigns has not been affirmatively proven.[^cite_note-273]

### Escalation of the Cold War

Further information: [Cold War (1979–1985)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1979%E2%80%931985\)) and [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine)

Reagan in the Oval Office, sitting with people from the Afghanistan-Pakistan region, February 1983

Reagan meeting with [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) leaders, 1983

Reagan ordered a massive defense buildup;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton19984-274] he revived the [B-1 Lancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-1_Lancer) program that had been rejected by the [Carter administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_administration),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008868-275] and deployed the [MX missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGM-118_Peacekeeper).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon200037-276] In response to Soviet deployment of the [SS-20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS-20), he oversaw [NATO](21133.md)'s deployment of the [Pershing missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pershing_missile) in Western Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000260-277] In 1982, Reagan tried to cut off the Soviet Union's access to [hard currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_currency) by impeding its proposed gas line to Western Europe. This hurt the Soviet economy, but also caused ill will among American allies in Europe who counted on the resulting revenue; he later retreated on this issue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraebnerBurnsSiracusa200829–31-278] In March 1983, Reagan introduced the [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative) (SDI) to protect the United States from space intercontinental ballistic missiles. He believed that this defense shield could protect the country from nuclear destruction in a hypothetical nuclear war with the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998131-279] There was much disbelief among the scientific community surrounding the program's scientific feasibility, leading opponents to dub the SDI "Star Wars",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015418-280] although Soviet leader [Yuri Andropov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Andropov) said it would lead to "an extremely dangerous path".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998132-281]



Reagan listening to Pakistani president [Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Zia-ul-Haq), 1982

In a 1982 address to the [British Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Parliament), Reagan said, "the march of freedom and democracy... will leave [Marxism–Leninism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism) on the [ash heap of history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash_heap_of_history)". Dismissed by the American press as "wishful thinking", [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher) called the address a "triumph".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000271–272-282] [David Cannadine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cannadine) says of Thatcher that "Reagan had been grateful for her interest in him at a time when the British establishment refused to take him seriously", with the two agreeing on "building up stronger defenses against Soviet Russia" and both believing in outfacing "what Reagan would later call '[the evil empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil_Empire_speech)'",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannadine201738-283] in reference to the Soviet Union, during a speech to the [National Association of Evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Evangelicals) in March 1983.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998130-237] After Soviet fighters downed [Korean Air Lines Flight 007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Air_Lines_Flight_007) in September, which included Congressman [Larry McDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_McDonald) and 61 other Americans, Reagan expressed outrage towards the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015420–421-284] The next day, reports suggested that the Soviets had fired on the plane by mistake.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998133-285] In spite of the harsh, discordant rhetoric,[^cite_note-286] Reagan's administration continued discussions with the Soviet Union on [START I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/START_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008868–869-287]

President Ronald Reagan Meeting with President Hosni Mubarak of Egypt in The Oval Office

Reagan meeting Egyptian President [Hosni Mubarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosni_Mubarak) in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), September 1983

Although the Reagan administration agreed with the communist government in China to [reduce the sale of arms to Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Communiqu%C3%A9s) in 1982,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000270-288] Reagan was the first US president to explicitly reject [containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment) and *détente*, instead advancing the idea that the Soviet Union could be defeated rather than managed through negotiation.[^cite_note-Knopf-289] His [covert aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone) to [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) forces through Pakistan against the Soviets has been given credit for assisting in ending the [Soviet occupation of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Afghanistan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergen200168-290] However, the United States was subjected [to blowback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blowback_\(intelligence\)) in the form of the [Taliban](30635.md) that opposed them in [the war in Afghanistan](19666611.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008883–884-291] In his [1985 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_State_of_the_Union_Address), Reagan proclaimed, "Support for [freedom fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_fighter) is self-defense."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000320-292] Through the [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine), his administration supported [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist) movements that fought against groups backed by the Soviet Union in an effort to [rollback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollback) Soviet-backed communist governments and reduce Soviet influence across the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKanet2006340Pach200678-293] The Reagan administration ignored [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations) in the countries they backed and held a narrow definition of [human rights](13831.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWawro2010381Søndergaard20204-294] Other human rights concerns include the [genocide in Guatemala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_under_R%C3%ADos_Montt),[^cite_note-295] as well as [mass killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truth_Commission_\(Chad\)) in [Chad](5488.md).[^cite_note-296]

### Invasion of Grenada

Main article: [United States invasion of Grenada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Grenada)

Reagan in the White House to discuss the Grenada situation with a bipartisan group of members of Congress, October 1983

Reagan discussing the Grenada situation with a bipartisan group of members of Congress, 1983

On October 19, 1983, [Maurice Bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Bishop) was overthrown and murdered by one of his colleagues. Several days later, Reagan ordered American forces to invade Grenada. Reagan cited a regional threat posed by a Soviet-Cuban military build-up and concern for the safety of hundreds of American medical students at [St. George's University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_University). Two days of fighting commenced, resulting in an American victory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2001187–188-297] While the invasion enjoyed public support in the United States, it was criticized internationally, with the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md) voting to censure the American government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015403-298] Cannon later noted that throughout Reagan's 1984 presidential campaign, the invasion overshadowed the [1983 Beirut barracks bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Beirut_barracks_bombings),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000393-299] which killed 241 Americans taking part in [an international peacekeeping operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Force_in_Lebanon) during the [Lebanese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawrence2021176-300]

### 1984 election

Main articles: [Ronald Reagan 1984 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_1984_presidential_campaign) and [1984 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election)

Results for the 1984 United States presidential election

1984 electoral vote results. Reagan won 525–13.

Reagan announced his reelection campaign on January 29, 1984, declaring, "America is back and standing tall".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998141-232] In February, his administration reversed the unpopular decision to send the [United States Marine Corps](17349325.md) to Lebanon, thus eliminating a political liability for him. Reagan faced minimal opposition in the Republican primaries,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2001188–191-301] and he and Bush accepted the nomination at [the Dallas convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Republican_National_Convention) in August.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoller2004369-302] In the general election, his campaign ran the commercial, "[Morning in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morning_in_America)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000452-303] At a time when the American economy was already recovering,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015452-222] former vice president [Walter Mondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Mondale)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015186-304] was attacked by Reagan's campaign as a "tax-and-spend Democrat", while Mondale criticized the deficit, the SDI, and Reagan's civil rights policy. However, Reagan's age induced his campaign managers to minimize his public appearances. Mondale's campaign believed that Reagan's age and mental health were issues before [the October presidential debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_debates).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998141–142-305]

Ronald Reagan's 2nd official portrait in 1985

Official portrait, 1985

Following Reagan's performance in the first debate where he struggled to recall statistics, his age was brought up by the media in negative fashion. Reagan's campaign changed his tactics for the second debate where he quipped, "I will not make age an issue of this campaign. I am not going to exploit, for political purposes, my opponent's youth and inexperience". This remark generated applause and laughter,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998142–143-306] even from Mondale. At that point, Broder suggested that age was no longer a liability for Reagan,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2001196-307] and Mondale's campaign felt that "the election was over".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998144-308] In November, Reagan won a landslide reelection victory with 59 percent of the popular vote and 525 electoral votes from 49 states. Mondale won 41 percent of the popular vote and 13 electoral votes from the District of Columbia and his home state of Minnesota.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoller2004373Cannon2003434-309]

### Response to the AIDS epidemic

Main article: [Ronald Reagan and AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_and_AIDS)

A 1987 ACT UP art installation quoting Reagan on AIDS with a blank slate to represent silence

Reagan has been criticized for his delayed and muted response to the AIDS epidemic. This 1987 art installation by [ACT UP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT_UP) quotes Reagan on AIDS with a blank slate, representing total silence.

The [AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_epidemic) began to unfold in 1981,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGellin199224-310] and AIDS was initially difficult to understand for physicians and the public.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazanjian2014353-311] As the epidemic advanced, according to White House physician and later physician to the president, brigadier general John Hutton, Reagan thought of AIDS as though "it was the measles and would go away". The October 1985 death of the President's friend and Hollywood celebrity, [Rock Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Hudson) affected Reagan's view; Reagan approached Hutton for more information on the disease. Still, between September 18, 1985, and February 4, 1986, Reagan did not mention AIDS in public.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000731-312]

In 1986, Reagan asked [C. Everett Koop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Everett_Koop) to develop a report on AIDS. Koop angered many evangelical conservatives, both in and out of the Reagan administration, by stressing the importance of sex education including condom usage in schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000731–733-313] A year later, Reagan, who reportedly had not read the report,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoop1991224-314] gave his first speech on the epidemic when 36,058 Americans had been diagnosed with AIDS, and 20,849 had died of it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShilts2000596-315] Reagan called for increased testing (including routine testing for marriage applicants) and mandatory testing of select groups (including federal prisoners).[^cite_note-316] Even after this speech, however, Reagan remained reluctant to publicly address AIDS.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000chapter_22-317]

Scholars and AIDS activists have argued that the Reagan administration largely ignored the [AIDS crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_crisis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELucas2009478–479-318][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrancis2012290-319][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKimShin2017518–519-320] [Randy Shilts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Shilts) and [Michael Bronski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Bronski) said that AIDS research was chronically underfunded during Reagan's administration, and Bronski added that requests for more funding by doctors at the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) were routinely denied.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShilts2000xxii-321][^cite_note-322] In a September 1985 press conference (shortly before Hudson publicly announced his AIDS diagnosis) Reagan called a government AIDS research program a "top priority", but also cited budgetary constraints.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015654,_656-323] Between the fiscal years of 1984 and 1989, federal spending on AIDS totaled \$5.6 billion. The Reagan administration had proposed \$2.8 billion during this time period, but pressure from congressional Democrats resulted in the larger amount.[^cite_note-324]

### Addressing apartheid

Reagan and Desmond Tutu shaking hands in the Oval Office, 1984

Reagan meeting with Bishop [Desmond Tutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desmond_Tutu) in the Oval Office, December 1984

Popular opposition to [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) increased during Reagan's first term in office and the [disinvestment from South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinvestment_from_South_Africa) movement achieved critical mass after decades of growing momentum. Criticism of apartheid was particularly strong on college campuses and among [mainline Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainline_Protestant) denominations.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326] President Reagan was opposed to divestiture because he personally thought, as he wrote in a letter to [Sammy Davis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Davis_Jr.), it "would hurt the very people we are trying to help and would leave us no contact within South Africa to try and bring influence to bear on the government". He also noted the fact that the "American-owned industries there employ more than 80,000 blacks" and that their employment practices were "very different from the normal South African customs".[^cite_note-327]

The Reagan administration developed [constructive engagement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constructive_engagement)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomson2008113-328] with the South African government as a means of encouraging it to gradually move away from apartheid and to give up its nuclear weapons [program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction).[^cite_note-329] It was part of a larger initiative designed to foster peaceful economic development and political change throughout southern Africa.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomson2008106–123-330] This policy, however, engendered much public criticism, and renewed calls for the imposition of stringent sanctions.[^cite_note-UngerVale-331] [Desmond Tutu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desmond_Tutu) described Reagan administration as "an unmitigated disaster for us blacks",[^cite_note-332] and Reagan himself as "a racist pure and simple".[^cite_note-333]

In response, Reagan announced the imposition of new sanctions on the South African government, including an [arms embargo](5974443.md) in late 1985.[^cite_note-334] These sanctions were seen as weak by anti-apartheid activists and as insufficient by the president's opponents in Congress.[^cite_note-UngerVale-331] In 1986, Congress approved the [Comprehensive Anti-Apartheid Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Anti-Apartheid_Act), which included tougher sanctions; Reagan's veto was overridden by Congress. Afterward, he remained opposed to apartheid and unsure of "how best to oppose it". Several European countries, as well as Japan, also imposed their sanctions on South Africa soon after.[^cite_note-335]

### Libya bombing

Main article: [1986 United States bombing of Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_bombing_of_Libya)

Reagan being briefed by the National Security Council Staff on the 1986 Libya air strike in the White House Situation Room. Seated with Reagan is George Shultz, William Casey, Don Regan, and Charles Gabriel.

Reagan receiving a briefing on the Libya bombing, 1986

Contentious relations between Libya and the United States under President Reagan were revived in the [West Berlin discotheque bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Berlin_discotheque_bombing) that killed two American soldiers and injured dozens of others on April 5, 1986. Stating that there was irrefutable evidence that Libya had a direct role in the bombing, Reagan authorized the use of force against the country. On April 14, the United States launched a series of [airstrikes](323167.md) on ground targets in Libya.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015530–531Woodard2012161-336] Thatcher allowed the [United States Air Force](32090.md) to use Britain's air bases to launch the attack, on the justification that the United Kingdom was supporting America's right to self-defense under Article 51 of the [Charter of the United Nations](88805.md).[^cite_note-337] The attack was, according to Reagan, designed to halt [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md)'s "ability to export terrorism", offering him "incentives and reasons to alter his criminal behavior".[^cite_note-338] The attack was condemned by many countries; by an overwhelming vote, the United Nations General Assembly adopted a resolution to condemn the attack and deem it a violation of the Charter and international law.[^cite_note-339]

### Iran–Contra affair

Main article: [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair)

Reagan in the Cabinet Room to receive the Tower Commission Report on the Iran–Contra affair, February 1987

Reagan receiving the [Tower Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_Commission) on the [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair), 1987

Reagan authorized [William J. Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_J._Casey) to arm the [Contras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contras), fearing that Communists would take over Nicaragua if it remained under the leadership of the [Sandinistas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinistas). Congress passed the 1982 [Boland Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boland_Amendment), prohibiting the CIA and Department of Defense from using their budgets to provide aid to the Contras. Still, the Reagan administration raised funds for the Contras from private donors and foreign governments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeisberg2016128–129-340] When Congress learned that the CIA had secretly placed [naval mines](22102.md) in Nicaraguan harbors, Congress passed a second Boland Amendment that barred granting any assistance to the Contras.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005208–209-341] By mid-1985, [Hezbollah](13919.md) began to [take American hostages in Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanon_hostage_crisis), holding seven of them in reaction to the United States' support of Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015488–491-342]

Reagan procured the release of seven American hostages held by Hezbollah by selling American arms to Iran, then engaged in the Iran–Iraq War, in hopes that Iran would pressure Hezbollah to release the hostages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeisberg2016129–134-343] The Reagan administration sold over 2,000 missiles to Iran without informing Congress; Hezbollah released four hostages but captured an additional six Americans. On [Oliver North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_North)'s initiative, the administration redirected the proceeds from the missile sales to the Contras.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeisberg2016129–134-343] The transactions were exposed by *[Ash-Shiraa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash-Shiraa)* in early November 1986. Reagan initially denied any wrongdoing, but on November 25, he announced that [John Poindexter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Poindexter) and North had left the administration and that he would form the [Tower Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_Commission) to investigate the transactions. A few weeks later, Reagan asked a panel of federal judges to appoint [a special prosecutor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Office_of_Special_Counsel) who would conduct a separate investigation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005210–211-344]

The Tower Commission released a report in February 1987 confirming that the administration had traded arms for hostages and sent the proceeds of the weapons sales to the Contras. The report laid most of the blame on North, Poindexter, and [Robert McFarlane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_McFarlane_\(American_politician\)), but it was also critical of [Donald Regan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Regan) and other White House staffers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015646–649-345] Investigators did not find conclusive proof that Reagan had known about the aid provided to the Contras, but the report noted that Reagan had "created the conditions which made possible the crimes committed by others" and had "knowingly participated or acquiesced in covering up the scandal".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005211–212-346] The affair damaged the administration and raised questions about Reagan's competency and the wisdom of conservative policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow2015202–204-347] The administration's credibility was also badly damaged on the international stage as it had violated its own arms embargo on Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015653,_674-348]

### USS *Stark* incident

&#x20;In the context of the [Tanker War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanker_War) on May 17, 1987, an Iraqi fighter jet hit the [USS *Stark*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Stark) with two [Exocet missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exocet_missiles), killing 37 sailors.[^cite_note-usni3-349][^cite_note-nhhc1-350] Three days later, Reagan declared a "policy of self-defense" would now be ordered, as he accepted Iraq's official apology:[^cite_note-ct2-351]

> Our ships are deployed in the Persian Gulf in order to protect U.S. interests and maintain free access and maintain [freedom of navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_navigation) and access to the area's oil supplies. It is a vital mission, but our ships need to protect themselves and they will. \[From now on] if aircraft approach any of our ships in a way that appears hostile, there is one order of battle. Defend yourselves. Defend American lives.. We're going to do what has to be done to keep the Persian Gulf open. It's international waters. No country there has a right to try and close it off and take it for itself. And the villain in the piece really is Iran. And so they're delighted with what has just happened.[^cite_note-crim1-352]

### Soviet decline and thaw in relations

Further information: [Cold War (1985–1991)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1985%E2%80%931991\))

Mikhail Gorbachev and Ronald Reagan signing the Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty in the East Room, December 1987

Mikhail Gorbachev and Reagan signing the [INF Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INF_Treaty), 1987

Although the Soviets did not accelerate military spending in response to Reagan's military buildup,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFischer20198-353] their enormous military expenses, in combination with [collectivized agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_Soviet_Union) and inefficient [planned manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy), were a heavy burden for the [Soviet economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_economy). At the same time, the prices of oil, the primary source of Soviet export revenues, fell to one third of the previous level in 1985. These factors contributed to a stagnant economy during the tenure of [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) as Soviet leader.[^cite_note-Gaidar-354]



President Ronald Reagan giving a speech at [Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University) in the [USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USSR), 1988

Reagan's foreign policy towards the Soviets wavered between [brinkmanship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brinkmanship) and cooperation.[^cite_note-355] Reagan appreciated Gorbachev's revolutionary change in the direction of the Soviet policy and shifted to diplomacy, intending to encourage him to pursue substantial arms agreements.[^cite_note-Knopf-289] They held [four summit conferences between 1985 and 1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Soviet_Union%E2%80%93United_States_summits#Cold_War_\(1985%E2%80%931991\)).[^cite_note-356] Reagan believed that if he could persuade the Soviets to allow for more democracy and free speech, this would lead to reform and the end of communism.[^cite_note-357] The critical summit was in [Reykjavík in 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_Summit), where they agreed to abolish all nuclear weapons. However, Gorbachev added the condition that SDI research must be confined to laboratories during the ten-year period when disarmament would take place. Reagan refused, stating that it was defensive only and that he would share the secrets with the Soviets, thus failing to reach a deal.[^cite_note-358]

In June 1987, Reagan addressed Gorbachev during a speech at the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall), demanding that he "[tear down this wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tear_down_this_wall)". The remark was ignored at the time, but after the wall [fell in November 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall), it was retroactively recast as a soaring achievement.[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] In December, Reagan and Gorbachev met again at [the Washington Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Summit_\(1987\))[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow2015234–235-362] to sign the [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty), committing to the total abolition of their respective short-range and medium-range missile stockpiles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005215-363] The treaty established an inspections regime designed to ensure that both parties honored the agreement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERossinow2015236-364] In May 1988, the US Senate overwhelmingly voted in favor of ratifying the treaty,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2005216-365] providing a major boost to Reagan's popularity in the aftermath of the Iran–Contra affair. A new era of trade and openness between the two powers commenced, and the United States and Soviet Union cooperated on international issues such as the Iran–Iraq War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008897–898-366]

## Post-presidency (1989–2004)

Reagan and Gorbachev relaxing at Rancho del Cielo in May 1992. Reagan gave Gorbachev a white cowboy hat, which he wore backwards.

Reagan and Gorbachev at Rancho del Cielo, 1992

The Reagans and Newport News Shipbuilding chairman and CEO William Frick standing behind a model of the aircraft carrier USS Ronald Reagan, 1996

Nancy and Ronald Reagan with a model of [USS *Ronald Reagan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Ronald_Reagan), 1996

Upon leaving the presidency on January 20, 1989, at the age of 77, Reagan became the oldest president at the end of his tenure. This distinction eventually passed to president [Joe Biden](145422.md), who was 82 years old when he left office in 2025.[^cite_note-NYT01202021-367][^cite_note-Bloomberg01192021-368]

In retirement, Ronald and Nancy Reagan lived at [668 St. Cloud Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/668_St._Cloud_Road) in [Bel Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bel_Air,_Los_Angeles), in addition to [Rancho del Cielo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rancho_del_Cielo) in [Santa Barbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Barbara,_California).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012180-369] He received [multiple awards and honors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_accolades_received_by_Ronald_Reagan)[^cite_note-370] in addition to generous payments for speaking engagements. In 1989 he supported repealing the [Twenty-second Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-second_Amendment)'s presidential term limits. In 1991, the [Ronald Reagan Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Presidential_Library) opened. Reagan also addressed the [1992 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Republican_National_Convention) "to inspire allegiance to the party regulars",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012181–182-371] and favored a [constitutional amendment](261586.md) requiring a balanced budget.

### Support for Brady Bill

Reagan publicly favored the [Brady Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brady_Bill), drawing criticism from gun control opponents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015717–718-372] In 1989, in his first public appearance after leaving office and shortly after the [Stockton schoolyard shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockton_schoolyard_shooting), he stated: "I do not believe in taking away the right of the citizen to own guns for sporting, for hunting, and so forth, or for home defense. But I do believe that an [AK-47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47), a machine gun, is not a sporting weapon or needed for the defense of the home".[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374]

In March 1991, Reagan wrote an op-ed in the *New York Times*, titled "Why I'm for the Brady Bill".[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] In May 1994, Reagan, [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford), and [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) sent a letter to House members, urging them to support the controversial [Federal Assault Weapons Ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assault_Weapons_Ban).[^cite_note-377]

### Alzheimer's disease

Reagan's final public speech occurred on February 3, 1994, during a tribute to him in Washington, D.C.; his last major public appearance was at the [funeral of Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Richard_Nixon#Funeral_service) on April 27, 1994.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012181–182-371] In August 1994, Reagan was diagnosed with [Alzheimer's disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alzheimer's_disease), which he announced through a handwritten letter in November.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000xiv-378] There was speculation over how long he had demonstrated symptoms of mental degeneration,[^cite_note-379] but lay observations that he suffered from Alzheimer's while still in office have been disputed by medical experts.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-sr-382] His doctors said that he first began exhibiting overt symptoms of the illness in late 1992[^cite_note-NYT_2004/06/15-383] or 1993.[^cite_note-sr-382] Over time, the disease destroyed Reagan's mental capacity. By 1997, he was reported to recognize few people other than his wife, though he continued to walk through parks and on beaches, play golf, and visit his office in nearby [Century City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_City).[^cite_note-sr-382] Eventually, his family decided that he would live in quiet semi-isolation with his wife.[^cite_note-384] By the end of 2003, Reagan had lost his ability to speak and was mostly confined to his bed, no longer able to recognize family members.[^cite_note-385]

## Death and funeral

Main article: [Death and state funeral of Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ronald_Reagan)



Reagan [lying in state](1505399.md) in the [US Capitol rotunda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Capitol_rotunda)



The tomb of Reagan and his wife, [Nancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Reagan), in October 2025

Reagan died of [pneumonia](52135.md), complicated by Alzheimer's,[^cite_note-Neuman-386] at [his home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/668_St._Cloud_Road) in Los Angeles, on June 5, 2004.[^cite_note-DrehleReaganDies-387] President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) called Reagan's death "a sad hour in the life of America".[^cite_note-Neuman-386] His public funeral was held in the [Washington National Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_National_Cathedral),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015731-388] where [eulogies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eulogies) were given by [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher), [Brian Mulroney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Mulroney), George H. W. Bush, and George W. Bush.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012184-389] Other world leaders attended including [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015732-390] Reagan was interred at [his presidential library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Presidential_Library).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012184-389]

## Legacy

See also: [List of things named after Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_things_named_after_Ronald_Reagan) and [Cultural depictions of Ronald Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_Ronald_Reagan)

A view of the Ronald Reagan Presidential Library from the south

The [Ronald Reagan Presidential Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Presidential_Library) in [Simi Valley, California](18593507.md)

### Approval ratings

Similar to previous presidents, Reagan began his presidency with [approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Approval_rating) greater than 50 percent,[^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392] peaking above 70 percent shortly after [his attempted assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Ronald_Reagan),[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] before declining by the end of his first year.[^cite_note-g5ugg5-395] Afterwards, his ratings fluctuated in the mid-30s and mid-40s in his second and third years,[^cite_note-g5ugg5-395][^cite_note-396] which has been attributed to the [1981–1982 recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_1980s_recession_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-g5ugg5-395] His approval ratings rebounded after the [invasion of Grenada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Grenada)[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] and by mid-1984 his approval rating neared 60 percent.[^cite_note-400] In the first two years of his second term, his approval ratings were consistently above 60 percent[^cite_note-401] but declined during the Iran–Contra scandal,[^cite_note-402] before beginning to recover in mid-1987.[^cite_note-403] In the [Gallup poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll), Reagan finished his presidency with an approval rating of 63 percent, the third highest for a departing president in history, behind only [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) and [Bill Clinton](3356.md), both of whom finished at 66 percent.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405]

In 1990, a year after he left office, a Gallup survey found that 54 percent of Americans said they approved of the overall job Reagan did as president.[^cite_note-406] The number of Americans who approved of the Reagan administration declined to 48 percent in 1992[^cite_note-407] but rebounded two years later to 52 percent.[^cite_note-408] In recent years, favorability of Reagan's presidency reached its highest ever: 71 percent approval in 2006;[^cite_note-vr33v55-409] 74 percent in 2010;[^cite_note-410] 72 percent in 2018;[^cite_note-411] and 69 percent in 2023.[^cite_note-uiwv3-412] He is often found to be second-most popular president since [World War II](32927.md), with only [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) having higher ratings.[^cite_note-vr33v55-409][^cite_note-uiwv3-412]

### Historical reputation



Reagan's 2016 coin from the [Presidential dollar coins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_dollar_coins) series

In 2008, British historian M. J. Heale summarized that scholars had reached a broad consensus in which "Reagan rehabilitated conservatism, turned the country to the right, practiced a '[pragmatic conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pragmatic_conservatism)' that balanced ideology with the constraints of government, revived faith in the presidency and American self-respect, and contributed to critically ending the Cold War",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenry2009933–934-413] which ended with the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000759Brands2015720-414][^cite_note-415] Many conservative and liberal scholars have agreed that Reagan has been the most influential president since Roosevelt, leaving his imprint on American politics, diplomacy, culture, and economics through his effective communication of his conservative agenda and pragmatic compromising.[^cite_note-416] During the initial years of Reagan's post-presidency, historical rankings [placed his presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States) in the twenties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatterson2003360Nichols2012282-417] Throughout the 2000s and 2010s, his presidency was often placed in the top ten.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENichols2012284Johns20151–2-418][^cite_note-419]

Many proponents, including his Cold War contemporaries,[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKupelian201070Fallon2017182Hampson2018230-421] believe that his defense policies, economic policies, military policies, and hard-line rhetoric against the Soviet Union and communism, together with his summits with Gorbachev, played a significant part in ending the Cold War.[^cite_note-American_Dreamer-422][^cite_note-Knopf-289] Jeffrey Knopf argues that while Reagan's practice of referring to the Soviet Union as "evil" probably made no difference to the Soviet leaders, it possibly gave encouragement to Eastern European citizens who opposed their communist regimes.[^cite_note-Knopf-289] [President Truman's policy of containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine) is also regarded as a force behind the fall of the Soviet Union, and the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan undermined the Soviet system itself.[^cite_note-Reagan's_role_exaggerated-423] Nevertheless, [Melvyn P. Leffler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melvyn_P._Leffler) called Reagan "Gorbachev's minor, yet indispensable partner, setting the framework for the dramatic changes that neither anticipated happening anytime soon".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeffler202137-424]

Critics, for example [Paul Krugman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Krugman), note Reagan's tenure as having begun a period of increased income inequality, sometimes called the "[Great Divergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Divergence_\(inequality\))". Krugman also views Reagan as having initiated the ideology of the current-day Republican Party, which he feels is led by "radicals" who seek to "undo the twentieth century" gains in income equality and unionization.[^cite_note-425] Others, such as Nixon's [Secretary of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Commerce) [Peter G. Peterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_G._Peterson), also criticize what they feel was not just Reagan's fiscal irresponsibility, but also the ushering in of an era where tax cutting "became the GOP's core platform", with resulting deficits and GOP leaders (speciously in Peterson's opinion) arguing supply-side gains would enable the country to "grow" its way out of deficits.[^cite_note-426]

Reagan was known for storytelling and humor,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015734-427] which involved puns[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon200097-428] and self-deprecation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998204-429] Reagan also often emphasized [family values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values), despite being the first president to have been divorced.[^cite_note-430] He showed the ability to comfort Americans during the aftermath of the [Space Shuttle *Challenger* disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Shuttle_Challenger_disaster).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWoodard2012166-431] Reagan's ability to talk about substantive issues with understandable terms and to focus on mainstream American concerns earned him the laudatory moniker the "Great Communicator".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000751-432][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2015734-427] He also earned the nickname "Teflon President" in that public perceptions of him were not substantially tarnished by the [controversies that arose during his administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scandals_of_the_Reagan_administration).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPemberton1998112-433][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECannon2000182-434]

### Political influence

Reagan led [a new conservative movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_coalition), altering the political dynamic of the United States.[^cite_note-legacy-cnn-435] Conservatism became the dominant ideology for Republicans, displacing the party's faction of liberals and moderates.[^cite_note-436] Men began voting more Republican, and women began voting more Democrat – a gender distinction that has persisted.[^cite_note-legacy-cnn-435] He was supported by young voters, an allegiance that shifted many of them to the party.[^cite_note-437] He attempted to appeal to Black voters in 1980,[^cite_note-438] but would receive the lowest Black vote for a Republican presidential candidate at the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShull199340-439] Throughout Reagan's presidency, Republicans were unable to gain complete control of Congress.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeclo2008570-440]

The period of American history most dominated by Reagan and his policies (particularly on taxes, welfare, defense, the federal judiciary, and the Cold War) is known as the [Reagan era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_era), which suggests that the "Reagan Revolution" had a lasting impact on the United States in domestic and foreign policy. The presidencies of George H. W. Bush, Bill Clinton, and George W. Bush are often treated as an extension of the era.[^cite_note-441] Since 1988, [Republican presidential candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Republican_Party_presidential_tickets) have invoked [Reagan's policies and beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Ronald_Reagan).[^cite_note-agpi-442]
